Schrödinger evolution in a low-density random potential: annealed convergence to the linear Boltzmann equation for general semiclassical Wigner measures Research supported by EPSRC grant EP/S024948/1
Søren Mikkelsen

Abstract
We consider solutions of the time-dependent Schrödinger equation for a potential localised at the points of a Poisson process. We prove convergence of the phase-space distribution in the annealed Boltzmann-Grad limit to a semiclassical Wigner (or defect) measure and show that it is a solution of the linear Boltzmann equation. Our results hold for a large class of square-integrable initial data associated to Wigner measures, including Langragian states, WKB states and coherent states. This extends important previous work by Eng and Erdős.



1
Introduction

For over a century, the
linear Boltzmann equation
has served as a central model for macroscopic particle transport in low-density matter
[
11
]
. There are now several rigorous derivations of this integro-differential equation, starting from a single-particle Schrödinger equation with a suitably scaled random potential. These include the ground-breaking studies by Spohn
[
16
]
and by Erdős and Yau
[
6
]
, which concern the weak-coupling limit with potential rescaled by a multiplicative constant but otherwise fixed. We will here investigate the scaling considered in Eng and Erdős’ important paper
[
5
]
, which corresponds to the
low-density
or
Boltzmann-Grad limit
as in Lorentz’ original work
[
11
]
in the context of classical dynamics, cf. also
[
17
,
7
,
1
]
.

Eng and Erdős investigate the Schrödinger equation in dimension
d\geq 3
restricted to a box with sides
L
, for a potential that is the superposition of
N
identical potentials localised at uniformly distributed points in the box. The relevant limits are
N,L\to\infty
so that the particle density
\rho=N/L^{d}
is fixed, followed by the small wave length limit
\hbar\to 0
where the localisation length of each potential is taken to be of the same order. The latter assumption leads to a Boltzmann equation whose collision kernel is given by the full quantum mechanical
T
-matrix, rather than the first-order Born approximation as in the case of the weak-coupling limit. The convergence to the linear Boltzmann equation is established for Husimi functions of the solution of the Schrödinger equation, whose initial condition is a specific Lagrangian state with sufficiently smooth weight and linear phase.

The main novelty of the results in the present work is two fold. Firstly we will allow a significantly more general class of
L^{2}
initial states than
[
5
]
, and formulate the convergence in terms of semiclassical Wigner measures instead of Husimi functions. This larger class will even include singular initial states. In the case of the weak-coupling limit, where the potential is given by a random field, the extension to such general initial conditions was given by Breteaux in
[
2
]
.

Secondly we remove the box and work with the Schrödinger equation in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, assuming that the potential is localised at the points of a Poisson process of intensity
\rho
. The advantage of this setup over the box is that it allows not only a discussion of the annealed limit (convergence on average over the scatterer configuration) but also the quenched limit (almost sure convergence) which we will discuss in a forthcoming paper.
In the classical dynamics setting, this is precisely the distinction between the works of Gallavotti
[
7
]
, Spohn
[
17
]
(annealed) on one hand and Boldrighini, Bunimovich and Sinai
[
1
]
(quenched) on the other. As in the work of Eng and Erdős we will restrict our attention to the case
d=3
. However most of our estimates hold for all
d\geq 3
.
In Section
1.5
we will give an outline of the paper and a comparison to previous works on this type of problem.

It should be noted that, although the linear Boltzmann equation breaks down in the case of periodic potentials, the same low-density scaling limit as in the random setting leads to new limit processes in both the classical and quantum setting. For details see
[
3
,
13
,
12
]
and
[
8
,
9
]
, respectively. However if damping is introduced into the microscopic equations convergence to the linear Boltzmann equation holds even for the periodic case. For details see
[
10
]
.

1.1
Schrödinger equation in a low density potential

Our setup is as follows. We consider the Hamilton operator

H_{\hbar,\lambda}=-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\Delta+\lambda W_{r}(x),

(1.1)

where
\hbar>0
is a scaling parameter (Planck’s constant measured in suitable units),
\Delta
is the standard Laplacian,
\lambda\geq 0
is the coupling constant, and the potential
W_{r}(x)
is the superposition of single site potentials
V_{r,x_{j}}
located at the points
x_{j}
of a locally finite set
\mathcal{X}
,

W_{r}(x)=\sum_{x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}}V_{r,x_{j}}(x).

More precisely,

V_{r,z}(x)=V\bigg(\frac{x-r^{1-1/d}z}{r}\bigg)

(1.2)

is the
r
-scaled single-site potential
V
localised at the point
r^{1-1/d}z
, with
V
a non-negative function in the Schwartz class
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Throughout this paper the point set
\mathcal{X}
is assumed to be a realisation of a Poisson point process with constant intensity
\rho
. We will implicitly assume we have a sufficiently rich probability space
(\Omega,\Sigma,\mathbb{P})
on which the point process is defined. The scaling of the scatterer locations
x_{j}
by
r^{1-1/d}
relative to the potential scaling by
r
ensures that the mean free path length remains constant as
r\to 0
. This scaling corresponds precisely to the Boltzmann-Grad limit.
In this setting, for every given
\hbar\in(0,\hbar_{0}]
, the Hamiltonian
H_{\hbar,\lambda}
is defined as the unique self-adjoint Friedrichs extension of the associated form
\mathbb{P}
-almost surely (see Section
2
for details).

The
T
-operator
T(E)
for the quantum mechanical scattering in the single-site potential
V
is defined as the limit

T(E)=\lim_{\gamma\to 0_{+}}T^{\gamma}(E),\qquad T^{\gamma}(E)=\lambda V+\lambda^{2}V\frac{1}{E-(-\frac{1}{2}\Delta+\lambda V)+i\gamma}V,

(1.3)

with
V
denoting the multiplication operator by the function
V
.
An important quantity is the kernel of
T(E)
in momentum representation with
E=\frac{1}{2}p^{2}
, which we denote by
\hat{T}(p,q)
. This “
T
-matrix” is related to the scattering matrix
S(p,q)
by the relation

S(p,q)=\delta(p-q)-2\pi i\,\delta(\tfrac{1}{2}p^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}q^{2})\,\hat{T}(p,q).

The unitarity of
S
implies the “optical theorem”

\im\hat{T}(q,q)=\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\delta(\tfrac{1}{2}p^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}q^{2})|\hat{T}(p,q)|^{2}\,dp.

(1.4)

As in the work of Eng and Erdős
[
5
]
, we will assume that
r
is of the same order as
\hbar
as this will allow us to observe the full quantum mechanical scattering process in the limit. To simplify notation we set in the following (without loss of generality)
\hbar=r
.

We consider solutions
\varphi(t,x)
of the Schrödinger equation

\begin{cases}i\hbar\partial_{t}\varphi(t,x)=H_{\hbar,\lambda}\varphi(t,x)\\
\varphi(0,x)=\varphi_{0}(x)\end{cases}

(1.5)

with initial data
\varphi_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
The solution of this initial value problem can be written as

\varphi(t,x)=U_{\hbar,\lambda}(t)\varphi_{0}(x),\qquad U_{\hbar,\lambda}(t)=e^{-it\hbar^{-1}H_{\hbar,\lambda}}.

(1.6)

A key point of our work is to choose
\hbar
-dependent initial states
\varphi_{0}
, whose phase space distribution converges to a limit measure as
\hbar\to 0
. We refer to the measures that can arise in this way as
semiclassical Wigner measures
.

1.2
Semiclassical Wigner measures

Denote by
\OpW(a)
the Weyl-quantised
\hbar
-pseudo-differential operator corresponding to the classical observable, or symbol,
a\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
. That is,

\OpW(a)\psi(x)=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}e^{i\hbar^{-1}\langle x-y,p\rangle}a(\tfrac{x+y}{2},p)\psi(y)\,dydp,

(1.7)

for
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
; cf.
[
15
,
18
]
. When the symbol
a
is assumed to be in
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
we have from
[
18
, Theorem 4.21]
that the operator
\OpW(a)
extends to a bounded operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. In particular, the operator norm is uniformly bounded in
\hbar
, i.e.

\sup_{\hbar\in(0,\hbar_{0}]}\lVert\OpW(a)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}<\infty.

A
semiclassical Wigner measure

\mu
(also known as
defect measure
) associated to a family of states
\{\psi_{\hbar}\}_{\hbar}
is a Radon measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
such that, for all
a\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
,

\langle\OpW(a)\psi_{\hbar},\psi_{\hbar}\rangle\to\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}a\,d\mu

(1.8)

along some subsequence of
\hbar\to 0
. Note that the standard assumption in the literature is usually “for all
a\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
”, but we will here need the stronger “for all
a\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
”. The Wigner measure may in general depend on the subsequence and not be unique. We will ensure uniqueness in the following by considering suitable subsequences
\{\psi_{\hbar}\}_{\hbar\in I}
. We say a semiclassical family is
uniform
in
\mathcal{H}_{\hbar}^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
if

\sup_{\hbar\in I}\|\psi_{\hbar}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\hbar}^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\infty,

where
n\in\mathbb{N}
and the space
\mathcal{H}_{\hbar}^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the semiclassical Sobolev space and is given by

\mathcal{H}_{\hbar}^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\Big\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,\Big|\,\sup_{|\alpha|\leq n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|(-i\hbar\partial_{x})^{\alpha}f(x)|^{2}\,dx<\infty\Big\}.

The following table shows examples of uniform semiclassical families
\{\psi_{\hbar}\}_{\hbar\in(0,1]}
and their (unique) Wigner measures
\mu
; cf.
[
18
, pp. 102-104]
:

Type

\psi_{\hbar}(x)

d\mu(x,p)

Lagrangian

w(x)\exp(i\hbar^{-1}\langle x,p_{0}\rangle)

|w(x)|^{2}\delta(p-p_{0})\,dx\,dp

Lagrangian

\hbar^{-d/2}w(\hbar^{-1}(x-x_{0}))

\delta(x-x_{0})|\hat{w}(p)|^{2}\,dx\,dp

WKB

w(x)\exp(i\hbar^{-1}S(x))

|w(x)|^{2}\delta(p-\partial_{x}S(x))\,dx\,dp

Coherent

(\pi\hbar)^{-d/4}\exp(i\hbar^{-1}\langle x-x_{0},p_{0}+i2(x-x_{0})\rangle)

\delta(x-x_{0})\delta(p-p_{0})\,dx\,dp

For the Lagrangian states we assume
w\in\mathcal{H}_{1}^{5d+5}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
; for the WKB state we assume in addition that
w
and
S
are smooth and
w
has compact support.

1.3
The linear Boltzmann equation

The linear Boltzmann equation with initial data
f_{0}
is given by

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\partial_{t}f(t,x,p)+\langle p,\nabla_{x}f(t,x,p)\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[\Sigma(p,q)f(t,x,q)-\Sigma(q,p)f(t,x,p)]\,dq\\
f(0,x,p)=f_{0}(x,p),\end{cases}

(1.9)

where
\Sigma(p,q)
is the collision kernel of the underlying microscopic single-site scattering process. The physical interpretation of
f(t,x,p)
is that of a particle density in position
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and momentum
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
at time
t
of a gas of non-interacting particles in a low-density medium.

The solution of the linear Boltzmann equation (
1.9
) can be expressed as the collision series

f(t,x,p)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}f^{(n)}(t,x,p),

(1.10)

where

\displaystyle f^{(n)}(t,x,q_{0})=\int_{[0,t]_{\leq}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{nd}}

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Sigma(q_{i},q_{i-1})\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}e^{-(s_{i-1}-s_{i})\Sigma_{\text{\rm tot}}(q)}

(1.11)

\displaystyle\times f_{0}(x-tq_{0}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}s_{i}(q_{i}-q_{i-1}),q_{n})\,d\bm{q}_{1,n}d\bm{s}_{n,1},

with the convention
s_{0}=t
,
s_{n+1}=0
, the notation

[0,t]_{\leq}^{n}=\{s_{n}\leq\cdots\leq s_{1}\,|\,s_{i}\in[0,t]\},

(1.12)

and the total scattering cross section

\Sigma_{\text{\rm tot}}(q)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Sigma(p,q)\,dp.

(1.13)

The expansion (
1.10
) defines a semigroup
\{\mathcal{L}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
of linear operators
\mathcal{L}_{t}:L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})\to L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
so that
f(t,x,p)=\mathcal{L}_{t}f_{0}(x,p)
. An important property of
\mathcal{L}_{t}
is that it preserves mass, i.e.,
\|\mathcal{L}_{t}f\|_{1}=\|f\|_{1}
for every measurable
f\geq 0
. We define the adjoint
\mathcal{L}_{t}^{*}
by

\int a(x,p)f(t,x,p)\,dx\,dp=\int[\mathcal{L}_{t}^{*}a](x,p)f_{0}(x,p)\,dx\,dp.

(1.14)

Given a Borel measure
\mu_{0}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, we define the measure
\mu_{t}
by

\int a(x,p)\,d\mu_{t}(x,p)=\int[\mathcal{L}_{t}^{*}a](x,p)\,d\mu_{0}(x,p).

(1.15)

We will say
the family of measures
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a solution of the linear Boltzmann equation with initial data
\mu_{0}
.

1.4
Main theorems

Define the norm

\lVert f\rVert_{p,q,n}=\max\bigg(\sup_{\beta,|\epsilon|\leq n}\lVert\langle x\rangle^{\beta}\partial^{\epsilon}f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\sup_{\beta,|\epsilon|\leq n}\lVert\langle x\rangle^{\beta}\partial^{\epsilon}f\rVert_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\bigg).

The standing assumption throughout this paper is that
\mathcal{X}
is a realisation of a Poisson point process with intensity
\rho
, the single-site potential
V\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is non-negative, and for a constant
C_{d}
only depending on the dimension the coupling constant satisfies

\lambda<\frac{1}{2C_{d}\lVert\hat{V}\rVert_{1,\infty,5d+5}}.

(1.16)

The probability and expectation of the Poisson process will be denoted by
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{E}
, respectively.
Under our assumptions, the Hamiltonian (
1.1
) is well defined as the unique self-adjoint Friedrichs extension of the associated form
\mathbb{P}
-almost surely (see Section
2.3
for details).

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
d=3
and suppose
\{\varphi_{\hbar}\}_{\hbar\in I}
is a uniform semiclassical family in
\mathcal{H}_{\hbar}^{5d+5}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with Wigner measure
\mu_{0}
,
and let
\varphi_{\hbar}(t)=U_{\hbar,\lambda}(t)\varphi_{\hbar}
.
Then, for any
t>0
,
a\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
, we have that

\mathbb{E}\langle\OpW(a)\varphi_{\hbar}(t),\varphi_{\hbar}(t)\rangle\to\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}a(x,p)\,d\mu_{t}(x,p),

(1.17)

for
\hbar\to 0
in
I
, where
\mu_{t}
is a solution of the linear Boltzmann equation with initial data
\mu_{0}
and collision kernel

\Sigma(p,q)=(2\pi)^{d+1}\rho\,\delta(\tfrac{1}{2}p^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}q^{2})|\hat{T}(p,q)|^{2}.

(1.18)

We restate Theorem
1.1
in the Heisenberg picture, where one considers the time evolution of observables rather than wave functions. The Heisenberg evolution of an initial observable
A_{\hbar}=\OpW(a)
is given by

A_{\hbar}(t)=U_{\hbar,\lambda}(t)\,A_{\hbar}\,U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-t).

(1.19)

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
d=3
and suppose
a\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
with
A_{\hbar}(t)
beeing the Heisenberg evolution of
A_{\hbar}=\OpW(a)
.
Then, for any
t>0
, and every uniform semiclassical family
\{\varphi_{\hbar}\}_{\hbar\in I}
in
\mathcal{H}_{\hbar}^{5d+5}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with Wigner measure
\mu_{0}
, we have that

\mathbb{E}\langle A_{\hbar}(t)\varphi_{\hbar},\varphi_{\hbar}\rangle\to\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}f(t,x,p)\,d\mu_{0}(x,p),

(1.20)

for
\hbar\to 0
in
I
, where
f(t,x,p)
is a solution of the linear Boltzmann equation with initial data
f(0,x,p)=a(x,p)
and collision kernel (
1.18
).

By unitarity,

\langle A_{\hbar}(t)\varphi_{\hbar},\varphi_{\hbar}\rangle=\langle\OpW(a)\varphi_{\hbar}(-t),\varphi_{\hbar}(-t)\rangle,

(1.21)

so Theorem
1.2
considers the reverse time direction of Theorem
1.1
in the quantum evolution. Likewise, the limit evolutions in Theorems
1.1
and Theorem
1.2
are governed by
\mathcal{L}_{t}^{*}a
and
\mathcal{L}_{t}a
, respectively. Thus the two theorems are in fact equivalent, and we will only give the proof of one of them: Theorem
1.2
.

A natural question would be to ask if the “smoothness” of the semiclassical family is really needed for the result to be true. In the next theorem we will see that if the semiclassical family is “well approximated” this assumption on smoothness can be relaxed. What we precisely mean by “well approximated” will be made precise in the statement of the theorem.
Firstly recall that a sequence of finite Borel measures
\{\mu_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
converges weakly to a finite Borel measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
if for all bounded continuous functions
f
it holds that

\int f\,d\mu_{n}\rightarrow\int f\,d\mu\qquad\text{as $n\rightarrow\infty$.}

(1.22)

With this we are now ready to state the generalisation.

Theorem 1.3
.

Suppose
d=3
and let
\{\varphi_{\hbar}\}_{\hbar\in I}
uniform semiclassical family in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with Wigner measure
\mu_{0}
. Assume that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists an uniform semiclassical family
\{\varphi_{\hbar}^{n}\}_{\hbar\in I}
in
\mathcal{H}^{5d+5}_{\hbar}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with Wigner measure
\mu_{0}^{n}
such that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{\hbar\in I}\lVert\varphi_{\hbar}-\varphi_{\hbar}^{n}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=0

(1.23)

and the sequence
\{\mu_{0}^{n}\}
converges weakly to
\mu_{0}
. Then the assertion of Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.2
holds for the semiclassical family
\{\varphi_{\hbar}\}_{\hbar\in I}
.

A proof of this theorem will be given in Section
13
. In this section we also give two examples of such “well approximated” semiclassical families. That such semiclassical families exists solidify the intuition that in this set up solutions to the linear Boltzmann equation are the universal limits no matter the initial conditions.

1.5
Outline of the paper and comparison to previous works

The remainder of the paper develops the various pieces needed for the proof of Theorem
1.2
. From here on we will mostly be working in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
d\geq 3
since most of our results are valid in all dimensions greater than or equal to
3
. In Section
2
we firstly fix the choice of Fourier transform and calculate the momentum representation of two operators, which we will use frequently in the following sections. Next we state and prove some integral estimates, which is fundamental in this approach. Lastly we prove that our Hamiltonian (
1.1
) is well defined under the stated assumptions on the potential, for almost every realisation of the Poisson process and some results on expectation of random phases.

The methods used in the proof of Theorem
1.2
are based on Duhamel expansions of the Schrödinger evolution in terms of multiple collision integrals.
We will not do a “full” expansion but truncate the expansion for certain collision patterns. This type of truncation was introduced by Erdős and Yau in their study of the weak-coupling limit
[
6
]
. For the expansions we will follow the methodology of Eng and Erdős
[
5
]
, but we need to expand to higher order in recollisions. We need these higher order terms due to our weakened assumptions on our initial states and to obtain improved error bounds. The specifics of the expansion and how we truncate it are given in Section
3
. The main result in Section
3
is Proposition
3.16
, which provides the explicit expansion derived from the Duhamel principle. The section is structured such that we first define the functions and operators that arise in the expansion and establish some of their key properties. Then Proposition
3.16
is stated and proven. The expansion we consider have a different form than the expansion in
[
5
]
. The two forms are equivalent and we can pass from one to the other by a change of variables in the time variables. This change of variables is given in Observation
4.2
. We state the expansion in this form as it is convenient for determining the limit, but we use the other expression for the a priori estimates.

A large part of this work is to estimate the different terms arising from the expansion. This is the content of
Sections

4
,
5
,
6
,
7
and
8
.
Due to the presence of the additional terms in the Duhamel expansion and the general initial states we can not directly apply the estimates from
[
5
]
. Instead we have modified the techniques and arguments used in
[
5
]
to obtain estimates for the different terms in the expansions. The main point of the modification is that we a priori need less derivatives of the initial states. Secondly we also obtain improved bounds from the modifications. One of these improvements is that for some estimates we get a factor
k
compared to a factor
k!
in
[
5
]
.
The modification is explained in more details in Remark
4.3
. Moreover, we will in
Sections

7
and
8
use a different technique compared to
[
5
]
for estimating some of the terms in the recollision error.

Since we are working in the framework of semiclassical/Wigner measures we need different techniques to prove the actual convergence of the main terms in the expansion compared to
[
5
]
. Especially we can not use that we are in a box of finite volume when we do regulations/renormalisations of the main terms. However, we will also use some of the techniques from
[
5
]
used to obtain the estimates on the terms in the Duhamel expansion from the previous sections. This is the content of
Sections

9
,
10
and
11
.

In Section
9
we prove two lemmas where we “regularise” the main terms in the expansion. The first one can be thought of as going to complex energies for the
T
-operator; the second is to remove some of the
\hbar
-dependence from the main terms. It is in this section where we start working with the form of the operators used in the statement of Proposition
3.16
. In
[
5
]
they also need to regularise or renormalise as they call it. In both cases this is done to get the main terms of the expansion into a form for which convergence can be proven.

In Section
10
we prove that the error terms do indeed go to zero in the semiclasical limit. The proof of the first Lemma is done by collecting the previous established norm bounds and verifying that under suitable choice of two numbers the error term convergence to zero. This is the same as in
[
5
]
. In the second Lemma we prove that the “main” terms with recollisions is of lower order and vanish in the semicalssical limit. Here we again use the modified techniques from
[
5
]
and some new techniques to handle certain terms.

Section
11
is devoted to working out semiclassical limit for the main terms in the expansion. This is done in a number of different steps including a further regularisation. In Section
12
we collect all previous results into a proof of Theorem
1.2
.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Fourier transform and momentum representation

We will use the following normalisation of the semiclassical Fourier transform and its inverse,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}\varphi(p)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-i\hbar^{-1}\langle x,p\rangle}\varphi(x)\,dx

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}\psi(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\hbar^{-1}\langle x,p\rangle}\psi(p)\,dp,

for
\varphi
and
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. In the case
\hbar=1
we will use the notation
\hat{\varphi}=\mathcal{F}\varphi=\mathcal{F}_{1}\varphi
. In the following we will also drop the subscript on the integrals if it is just “the right power” of
\mathbb{R}
. For some specific cases we will keep it though like in the statement of Lemma
2.1
, which is only valid in
3
-dimensions.

For an operator
A
we define its momentum representation by
\hat{A}=\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}A\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}
. We will here give three examples of kernels in momentum representation. We will later in the analysis be using all three. We have in the sense of distributions that the kernels in momentum representation are

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}W_{\hbar}\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}(p,q)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\sum_{x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}}e^{-i\hbar^{-1/d}\langle x_{j},p-q\rangle}\hat{V}(p-q),

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}e^{-it\hbar^{-1}(-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\Delta)}\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}(p,q)=\delta(p-q)e^{-it\hbar^{-1}\frac{1}{2}q^{2}}

and

\displaystyle K_{\hat{A}_{\hbar}}(p,q)=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}[a(\cdot,\tfrac{p+q}{2})](p-q).

Lastly we have the following Lemma for which a proof can be found in e.g.
[
4
]
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
z\in\mathbb{C}
be given such that
\im(z)\neq 0
. Then we have

\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{e^{i\langle x,p\rangle}}{\frac{1}{2}p^{2}-z}\,dp=\frac{e^{i|x|\sqrt{2z}}}{2\pi|x|},

(2.1)

where it is the branch of the square root giving a positive imaginary part.

2.2
Integral estimates

In the following the set of natural numbers (excluding zero) is denoted
\mathbb{N}
, and we also set
\mathbb{N}_{0}=\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
. For a collection of vectors
x_{l},x_{l+1},\dots,x_{j}
and scalars
s_{l},\dots,s_{j}
we define.

\bm{x}_{l,j}=(x_{l},\dots,x_{j}),\qquad\bm{x}_{l,j}^{+}=\sum_{i=l}^{j}x_{i},\qquad\bm{sx}_{l,j}^{+}=\sum_{i=l}^{j}s_{i}x_{i}.

Furthermore, will use the convention that for
m<n
that

\prod_{i=n}^{m}a_{i}=1\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sum_{i=n}^{m}a_{i}=0.

The following lemma is a version of the classical stationary phase argument. We have here included a proof since we will be using the same type of argumentation in later proofs.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{nd})
. Suppose
Q(\bm{t})
is an
nd\times nd
diagonal matrix with block form

Q(\bm{t})=\begin{pmatrix}t_{1}I_{d}&0&0&0\\
0&t_{2}I_{d}&0&0\\
\vdots&0&\ddots&0\\
0&\cdots&0&t_{n}I_{d}\end{pmatrix},

with
t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
and
I_{d}
is the
d\times d
identity matrix and finally let
\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{nd}
. Then

\displaystyle\Big|\int f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})e^{i\frac{1}{2}\langle Q(\bm{t})\bm{x},\bm{x}\rangle}e^{i\langle\bm{x},\bm{y}\rangle}\,d\bm{x}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\tilde{C}_{d}^{n}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\max(1,|t_{i}|)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\sup_{|\alpha_{1}|,\dots,|\alpha_{n}|\leq d+1}\lVert\partial_{x_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\partial_{x_{n}}^{\alpha_{n}}f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{nd})},

where the constant is only dependent on the dimension
d
and is given by

\tilde{C}_{d}=\left(\frac{\sqrt{\pi}d(d+1)^{(d+1)}\Gamma(\frac{d}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{d}{2}+1)\Gamma(\frac{d+1}{2})}\right).

Proof.

We start by dividing our integral into different cases depending on the absolute value of the
t
’s. We divide into the cases where
|t_{i}|\geq 1
and
|t_{i}|<1
. This gives us
2^{n}
different cases to consider and we denote each corresponding set by
B_{l}
. For each case the indices for which
|t_{i}|\geq 1
will be denoted by
J_{1}
and the remaining will be in
J_{2}
. With this in place we write the integral as

\int f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})e^{i\frac{1}{2}\langle Q(\bm{t})\bm{x},\bm{x}\rangle}e^{i\langle\bm{x},\bm{y}\rangle}\,d\bm{x}=\sum_{l=1}^{2^{n}}\int\bm{1}_{B_{l}}(\bm{t})f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})e^{i\frac{1}{2}\langle Q(\bm{t})\bm{x},\bm{x}\rangle}e^{i\langle\bm{x},\bm{y}\rangle}\,d\bm{x}.

(2.2)

We will decompose
Q(\bm{t})
into two different matrices depending on which indices is in
J_{1}
or
J_{2}
. By assumption we have that

\displaystyle\langle Q(\bm{t})\bm{x},\bm{x}\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}t_{i}^{d}x_{i}^{2}=\sum_{i\in J_{1}}t_{i}^{d}x_{i}^{2}+\sum_{i\in J_{2}}t_{i}^{d}x_{i}^{2}=\langle Q_{1}(\bm{t})\bm{x}_{J_{1}},\bm{x}_{J_{1}}\rangle+\langle Q_{2}(\bm{t})\bm{x}_{J_{2}},\bm{x}_{J_{2}}\rangle,

(2.3)

where
Q_{1}(\bm{t})
and
Q_{2}(\bm{t})
are diagonal square matrices. In particular we have that

|\det[Q_{1}(\bm{t})]|=\prod_{i\in J_{1}}|t_{i}|^{d}\geq 1.

Hence using decomposition (
2.3
) and writing the function
e^{i\frac{1}{2}\langle Q_{1}(\bm{t})\bm{x},\bm{x}\rangle}
as the inverse Fourier transform of its Fourier transform we get that

\displaystyle\int\bm{1}_{B_{l}}(\bm{t})f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})e^{i\frac{1}{2}\langle Q(\bm{t})\bm{x},\bm{x}\rangle}\,d\bm{x}={}

\displaystyle\prod_{i\in J_{1}}\frac{1}{(2\pi t_{i})^{\frac{d}{2}}}e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}\sgn(Q_{1}(\bm{t}))}\int\bm{1}_{B_{l}}(\bm{t})f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})e^{-i\frac{1}{2}\langle Q_{1}(\bm{t})^{-1}\bm{p}_{J_{1}},\bm{p}_{J_{1}}\rangle}

\displaystyle\times e^{i\frac{1}{2}\langle Q_{2}(\bm{t})\bm{x}_{J_{2}},\bm{x}_{J_{2}}\rangle}e^{i\langle\bm{p}_{J_{1}}+\bm{y}_{J_{1}},\bm{x}_{J_{1}}\rangle}e^{i\langle\bm{x}_{J_{2}},\bm{y}_{J_{2}}\rangle}\,d\bm{p}d\bm{x}.

To ensure integrability in the
p
variables we define the differential operator

L=\prod_{i\in J_{1}}\frac{1-i\langle p_{i},\nabla_{x_{i}}\rangle}{1+|p_{i}|^{2}}.

We observe that
Le^{i\langle\bm{p}_{J_{1}},\bm{x}_{J_{1}}\rangle}=e^{i\langle\bm{p}_{J_{1}},\bm{x}_{J_{1}}\rangle}
. Using this, we insert
L
,
d+1
times, and integrate by parts. This gives us the estimate

\displaystyle\Big|\int\bm{1}_{B_{l}}(\bm{t})f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})e^{i\frac{1}{2}\langle Q(\bm{t})\bm{x},\bm{x}\rangle}e^{i\langle\bm{x},\bm{y}\rangle}\,d\bm{x}\Big|\leq\sum_{|\alpha_{1}|,\dots,|\alpha_{j_{1}}|\leq d+1}\bm{1}_{B_{l}}(\bm{t})\prod_{i\in J_{1}}\frac{C(\alpha_{i})}{(2\pi|t_{i}|)^{\frac{d}{2}}}

(2.4)

\displaystyle\times\int|\partial_{\bm{x}_{J_{1}}}^{\bm{\alpha}_{J_{1}}}f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})|\prod_{i\in J_{1}}\frac{|p_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}|}{(1+|p_{i}|^{2})^{d+1}}\,d\bm{p}_{J_{1}}d\bm{x},

where we have used the notation
C(\alpha)=\binom{d+1}{(\alpha,d+1-\lvert\alpha\rvert)}
. We note that

\displaystyle\int\frac{|p_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}|}{(1+|p_{i}|^{2})^{d+1}}\,dp\leq\int\frac{1}{(1+|p_{i}|^{2})^{\frac{d+1}{2}}}\,dp_{i}=\frac{\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}d}{\Gamma(\frac{d}{2}+1)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{r^{d-1}}{(1+r^{2})^{\frac{d+1}{2}}}\,dr=\frac{\pi^{\frac{d+1}{2}}d\Gamma(\frac{d}{2})}{2\Gamma(\frac{d}{2}+1)\Gamma(\frac{d+1}{2})}.

(2.5)

Combining (
2.4
) and (
2.5
) we obtain the estimate

\displaystyle\Big|\int\bm{1}_{B_{l}}(\bm{t})f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})e^{i\frac{1}{2}\langle Q(\bm{t})\bm{x},\bm{x}\rangle}e^{i\langle\bm{x},\bm{y}\rangle}\,d\bm{x}\Big|\leq\left(\frac{\sqrt{\pi}d\Gamma(\frac{d}{2})}{2\Gamma(\frac{d}{2}+1)\Gamma(\frac{d+1}{2})}\right)^{j_{1}}

(2.6)

\displaystyle\times\sup_{|\alpha_{1}|,\dots,|\alpha_{n}|\leq d+1}\lVert\partial_{x_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\partial_{x_{n}}^{\alpha_{n}}f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{nd})}\sum_{|\alpha_{1}|,\dots,|\alpha_{j_{1}}|\leq d+1}\bm{1}_{B_{l}}(\bm{t})\prod_{i\in J_{1}}\frac{C(\alpha_{i})}{|t_{i}|^{\frac{d}{2}}}.

To obtain the desired estimate we first note for all
l
that

\displaystyle\bm{1}_{B_{l}}(\bm{t})\prod_{i\in J_{1}}\frac{1}{|t_{i}|^{\frac{d}{2}}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\max(1,|t_{i}|)^{\frac{d}{2}}}.

(2.7)

Secondly we note that

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha_{1}|,\dots,|\alpha_{j_{1}}|\leq d+1}\prod_{i=1}^{j_{1}}\binom{d+1}{(\alpha_{i},d+1-\lvert\alpha_{i}\rvert)}=\prod_{i=1}^{j_{1}}\sum_{|\alpha_{i}|\leq d+1}\binom{d+1}{(\alpha_{i},d+1-\lvert\alpha_{i}\rvert)}=(d+1)^{(d+1)j_{1}}.

(2.8)

Combing (
2.6
), (
2.7
) and (
2.8
) we get the estimate

\displaystyle\Big|\int\bm{1}_{B_{l}}(\bm{t})f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})e^{i\frac{1}{2}\langle Q(\bm{t})\bm{x},\bm{x}\rangle}e^{i\langle\bm{x},\bm{y}\rangle}\,d\bm{x}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{\tilde{C}_{d}}{2}\right)^{n}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\max(1,|t_{i}|)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\sup_{|\alpha_{1}|,\dots,|\alpha_{n}|\leq d+1}\lVert\partial_{x_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\partial_{x_{n}}^{\alpha_{n}}f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{nd})}.

which combined with (
2.2
) gives us the stated estimate.
∎

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\gamma\in(0,1]
be given. Then the following estimate holds

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1-e^{-\gamma t}}{\max(1,t^{\frac{3}{2}})}\,dt\leq 5\gamma^{\frac{1}{3}}.

Proof.

We start by setting
T=\gamma^{-\frac{2}{3}}
and write the integral as

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1-e^{-\gamma t}}{\max(1,t^{\frac{3}{2}})}\,dt=\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1-e^{-\gamma t}}{\max(1,t^{\frac{3}{2}})}\,dt+\int_{T}^{\infty}\frac{1-e^{-\gamma t}}{\max(1,t^{\frac{3}{2}})}\,dt.

(2.9)

For the first integral we use the estimate
1-e^{-\gamma t}\leq\gamma t
and obtain that

\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1-e^{-\gamma t}}{\max(1,t^{\frac{3}{2}})}\,dt\leq\gamma T\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{\max(1,t^{\frac{3}{2}})}\,dt\leq\gamma T\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\max(1,t^{\frac{3}{2}})}\,dt=3\gamma T=3\gamma^{\frac{1}{3}}.

(2.10)

For the second integral we use the trivial bound
1-e^{-\gamma t}\leq 1
and that
T\geq 1
and obtain that

\int_{T}^{\infty}\frac{1-e^{-\gamma t}}{\max(1,t^{\frac{3}{2}})}\,dt\leq\int_{T}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t^{\frac{3}{2}}}\,dt=2T^{-\frac{1}{2}}=2\gamma^{\frac{1}{3}}.

(2.11)

The stated estimate is obtained from combining (
2.9
), (
2.10
) and (
2.11
).
∎

We will in our analysis also need the following integral estimates contained in the next two lemmas. The proofs of both lemmas are given in the Appendix.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be given. Then the following estimates holds

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{\langle x\rangle^{d+1}|x-y|}\,dx\leq\frac{C}{\langle y\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

(2.12)

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
\zeta\in(0,1/2)
be given. Then the following estimates holds

\sup_{p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\langle\nu\rangle|\frac{1}{2}p^{2}+\nu+i\zeta|}\,d\nu\leq C|\log(\zeta)|.

(2.13)

\sup_{\nu\in\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\langle\nu\rangle}{\langle p\rangle^{d+1}|\frac{1}{2}p^{2}+\nu+i\zeta|}\,dp\leq C|\log(\zeta)|.

(2.14)

\sup_{q\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\nu\in\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{|p-q|}\frac{\langle\nu\rangle}{\langle p\rangle^{d+1}|\frac{1}{2}p^{2}+\nu+i\zeta|}\,dp\leq C|\log(\zeta)|.

(2.15)

\sup_{q\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\nu\in\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{\langle p-q\rangle^{d+1}|\frac{1}{2}p^{2}+\nu+i\zeta|}\,dp\leq C|\log(\zeta)|.

(2.16)

Where all constants are independent of
\zeta
, but they depend on the dimension.

The last integral estimate we will need is contained in the following Lemma and a proof of this Lemma can be found in
[
5
, Proposition 2.3]
. For sake of completness a proof can also be found in the appendix.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\zeta\in(0,1/2)
be given. Then the following estimate holds

\sup_{\nu,\tilde{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\langle\nu\rangle\langle\tilde{\nu}\rangle}{\langle p\rangle^{d+1}\langle p-q\rangle^{d+1}|\frac{1}{2}p^{2}+\nu+i\zeta||\frac{1}{2}(p-q)^{2}+\tilde{\nu}+i\zeta|}\,dp\leq\frac{C\log(\zeta)^{2}}{|q|+\zeta},

(2.17)

where the constant is independent of
\zeta
and
q
.

Remark 2.7
.

For applications of this Lemma we will use it in the following form

\sup_{\nu,\tilde{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\langle\nu\rangle\langle\tilde{\nu}\rangle}{\langle p\rangle^{d+1}\langle p-q\rangle^{d+1}|\frac{1}{2}p^{2}+\nu+i\zeta||\frac{1}{2}(p-q)^{2}+\tilde{\nu}+i\zeta|}\,dp\leq\frac{C\log(\zeta)^{2}}{|q|},

(2.18)

where this bound is only true almost everywhere with respect to the Lebesgue measure.

2.3
Almost sure local integrability of the potential and expectation of random phases

We are considering an operator of the form
H_{\hbar,\lambda}=-\hbar^{2}\Delta+\lambda W_{\hbar}
, where the potential is of the form

W_{\hbar}(x)=\sum_{x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}}V(\hbar^{-1}x-\hbar^{-1/d}x_{j}),

where
\mathcal{X}
is a Possion point process with intensity measure
\rho dx
and
V
is a measurable function. A first natural question is weather this operator is well defined and self-adjoint. One approach is to establish that
W_{\hbar}
is in
L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\mathbb{P}
-almost surely and is bounded from below. Then we can define the operator
\mathbb{P}
-almost surely as a Friedrichs extension of the quadratic form given by

\mathfrak{h}[f,g]=\int\hbar^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\partial_{x_{i}}f(x)\overline{\partial_{x_{i}}g(x)}+\lambda W_{\hbar}(x)f(x)\overline{g(x)}\;dx,\qquad f,g\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{h}),

where

\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{h})=\left\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lvert p\rvert^{2}\lvert\hat{f}(p)\rvert^{2}\;dp<\infty\text{ and }\int\lvert W_{\hbar}(x)\rvert\lvert f(x)\rvert^{2}\;dx<\infty\right\}.

In fact this Friedrichs extension will be self-adjoint and unique; cf.
[
14
]
.

For a start assume
V(\hbar^{-1}(x-\hbar^{1-1/d}\cdot))
to be in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\rho dx)
, uniformly for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. By Campbell’s theorem we have that

\mathbb{E}[|W_{\hbar}(x)|]\leq\mathbb{E}[\sum_{x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}}|V(\hbar^{-1}x-\hbar^{-1/d}x_{j})|]=\rho\int|V(\hbar^{-1}x-\hbar^{-1/d}y)|dy<\infty.

Then by the Markov inequality we get for
\varepsilon>0
that

\mathbb{P}(|W_{\hbar}(x)|>\varepsilon^{-1})\leq\varepsilon\mathbb{E}[|W_{\hbar}(x)|].

This gives us that the probability of the total potential being pointwise finite is
1
as long as the single site potential is integrable. This implies that the potential
\mathbb{P}
-almost surely will be finite on any countable collection of points. However, in the case of stationary Poisson point process (as we consider) the potential will not be in
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
almost surely. To see this let
\{U_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a collection of disjoint sets with volume one such that
\mathbb{R}^{d}=\cup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}U_{n}
. For a
k\in\mathbb{N}
consider the probability

\mathbb{P}(\exists n\in\mathbb{N}\,:\#(\mathcal{X}\cap U_{n})>k)=1-\mathbb{P}(\#(\mathcal{X}\cap U_{n})\leq k\,\forall n\in\mathbb{N}).

Since we assume that the sets have volume
1
we get for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
that

\mathbb{P}(\#(\mathcal{X}\cap U_{n})\leq k)=e^{-\rho}\sum_{j=0}^{k}\frac{\rho^{j}}{j!}<1.

Moreover, we assumed the sets are disjoint, hence we get by independence that

\mathbb{P}(\#(\mathcal{X}\cap U_{n})\leq k\,\forall n\in\mathbb{N})=\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}\big(e^{-\rho}\sum_{j=0}^{k}\frac{\rho^{j}}{j!}\big)^{l}=0.

Hence for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
we have that

\mathbb{P}(\exists n\in\mathbb{N}\,:\#(\mathcal{X}\cap U_{n})>k)=1.

This will imply that we cannot
\mathbb{P}
-almost surely have a uniformly bound on the potential. This is also the reason we assume our single site potentials is nonnegative, since otherwise we cannot verify almost surely that the full potential
W_{\hbar}
will be bounded from below. We will in the following lemma prove that the full potential will be in
L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
almost surely.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a Poisson point process with intensity
\rho
and let
V\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. For
\hbar\in(0,1]
define the function
W_{\hbar}(x)
by

W_{\hbar}(x)=\sum_{x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}}V(\hbar^{-1}x-\hbar^{-1/d}x_{j}).

(2.19)

Then for given
\hbar
we have that
W_{\hbar}(x)\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\mathbb{P}
-almost surely.

Proof.

First we observer by use of Campbell’s theorem and for any
\hbar>0
that

\mathbb{E}[\sum_{x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}}\langle\hbar^{-1/d}x_{j}\rangle^{-d-1}]=\hbar\int\langle x\rangle^{-d-1}\rho\,dx<\infty,

(2.20)

where we have used the notation
\langle x\rangle=(1+|x|^{2})^{1/2}
. This implies that

\sum_{x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}}\langle\hbar^{-1/d}x_{j}\rangle^{-d-1}<\infty\qquad\text{$\mathbb{P}$-a.s.}

To prove that
W_{\hbar}(x)\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\mathbb{P}
-almost surely, it is sufficient to prove that

\int_{B(0,R)}|W_{\hbar}(x)|\,dx<\infty\qquad\text{$\mathbb{P}$-a.s.}

(2.21)

for every
R>0
, where the
\mathbb{P}
-null set is independent of
R
. Let
R>0
be given. For
x\in B(0,\hbar^{-1}R)
we have that

\displaystyle\langle\hbar^{-1/d}x_{j}\rangle

\displaystyle=(1+|\hbar^{-1/d}x_{j}-x+x|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}

(2.22)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}(\langle\hbar^{-1/d}x_{j}-x\rangle+\hbar^{-1}R)\leq\sqrt{2}(1+\hbar^{-1}R)\langle\hbar^{-1/d}x_{j}-x\rangle.

This implies that

\frac{\langle\hbar^{-1/d}x_{j}\rangle}{\sqrt{2}(1+\hbar^{-1}R)}\leq\langle\hbar^{-1/d}x_{j}-x\rangle.

(2.23)

With this bound we have that

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,R)}|W_{\hbar}(x)|\,dx\leq{}

\displaystyle\hbar^{d}\sum_{x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}}\int_{B(0,\hbar^{-1}R)}|V(x-\hbar^{-1/d}x_{j})|\,dx

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}(1+\hbar^{-1}R)^{d+1}C\Vol(B(0,R))\sum_{x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}}\frac{1}{\langle\hbar^{-1/d}x_{j}\rangle^{d+1}}<\infty\quad\text{$\mathbb{P}$-a.s.}

where we have that the
\mathbb{P}
-null set is independent of
R
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Our next lemma is a result on the average of a random phase. To state the Lemma we will need some additional notation to keep track of the combinatorics involved in this problem. We will by
\mathcal{S}_{n}
denote the set of permutations of
n
elements and we let

\mathcal{A}(k,n)=\{\bm{\sigma}\in\{1,\dots,k\}^{n}\,|\,\sigma_{1}<\sigma_{2}<\cdots<\sigma_{n}\}.

(2.24)

We can now state the lemma.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
k_{1}\geq k_{2}
,
n\in\{0,\dots,k_{2}\}
,
\sigma^{1}\in\mathcal{A}(k_{1},n)
and
\sigma^{2}\in\mathcal{A}(k_{2},n)
. Suppose
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{(k_{1}+1)d})
,
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{(k_{2}+1)d})
and let
\kappa
be in
\mathcal{S}_{n}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{(\bm{x},\tilde{\bm{x}})\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\rho^{-1}\delta(x_{\sigma_{i}^{1}}-\tilde{x}_{\sigma_{\kappa(i)}^{2}})\int\prod_{m=1}^{k_{1}}e^{-i\langle\hbar^{-1/d}x_{m},p_{m}-p_{m-1}\rangle}\prod_{m=1}^{k_{2}}e^{i\langle\hbar^{-1/d}\tilde{x}_{m},q_{m}-q_{m-1}\rangle}f(\bm{p})g(\bm{q})\,d\bm{p}d\bm{q}\Big]

(2.25)

\displaystyle=\left(\rho\hbar(2\pi)^{d}\right)^{k_{1}+k_{2}-n}\int\Lambda_{n}(\bm{p},\bm{q},\sigma^{1},\sigma^{2},\kappa)f(\bm{p})g(\bm{q})\,d\bm{p}d\bm{q},

where the function
\Lambda_{n}(\bm{p},\bm{q},\sigma^{1},\sigma^{2},\kappa)
is given by

\displaystyle\Lambda_{n}(\bm{p},\bm{q},\sigma^{1},\sigma^{2},\kappa)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\delta(p_{\sigma^{1}_{i-1}}-q_{\sigma^{2}_{\kappa(i)-1}}-p_{\sigma^{1}_{n}}+q_{\sigma^{2}_{n}}-l^{\kappa}_{i}(\bm{q}_{\sigma^{2}}))\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}\prod_{m=\sigma^{1}_{i-1}+1}^{\sigma^{1}_{i}-1}\delta(p_{m}-p_{\sigma^{1}_{i-1}})\prod_{m=\sigma^{2}_{i-1}+1}^{\sigma^{2}_{i}-1}\delta(q_{m}-q_{\sigma^{1}_{i-1}}),

where we have used the notation
l^{\kappa}_{i}(\bm{q}_{\sigma^{2}})=\sum_{j=i+1}^{n+1}(q_{\sigma^{2}_{\kappa(j)-1}}-q_{\sigma^{2}_{\kappa(j-1)}})
.

Remark 2.10
.

Assume we are in the setting of Lemma
2.9
. For any
\kappa\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
we have that

q_{\sigma^{2}_{\kappa(1)-1}}+l^{\kappa}_{1}(\bm{q}_{\sigma^{2}})=q_{0}.

In the case where
\kappa=\mathrm{id}
we have that
l^{\kappa}_{i}(\bm{q}_{\sigma^{2}})=0
for all
i
. For all other
\kappa
, there is at least one
i
such that
l^{\kappa}_{i}(\bm{q}_{\sigma^{2}})\neq 0
.

We will later also need a slightly different version of the Lemma, where the function
\Lambda_{n}(\bm{p},\bm{q},\sigma^{1},\sigma^{2},\kappa)
will be given by

\displaystyle\Lambda_{n}(\bm{p},\bm{q},\sigma^{1},\sigma^{2},\kappa)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\delta(p_{\sigma^{1}_{i}}-q_{\sigma^{2}_{\kappa(i)}}-p_{\sigma^{1}_{1}}+q_{\sigma^{2}_{1}}-\tilde{l}^{\kappa}_{i}(\bm{q}))\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}\prod_{m=\sigma^{1}_{i-1}+1}^{\sigma^{1}_{i}-1}\delta(p_{m}-p_{\sigma^{1}_{i}})\prod_{m=\sigma^{2}_{i-1}+1}^{\sigma^{2}_{i}-1}\delta(q_{m}-q_{\sigma^{1}_{i}}),

where we have used the notation
\tilde{l}^{\kappa}_{i}(\bm{q})=\sum_{j=1}^{i}(q_{{\kappa(j-1)}}-q_{{\kappa(j)-1}})
. To obtain this other statement the final change of variables in the following proof needs to be change to
x_{n}\mapsto x_{n}
and
x_{i}\mapsto x_{i}-x_{i+1}
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
.

Proof.

We will in the proof use the notation
\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{X},k_{1},k_{2})
for the integral we take expectation over in the lefthand side of (
2.25
). Using Campbell’s theorem we get that the average is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{X},k_{1},k_{2})]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\rho^{k_{1}+k_{2}-n}\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}\delta(x_{\sigma_{i}^{1}}-\tilde{x}_{\sigma_{\tau(i)}^{2}})\prod_{m=1}^{k_{1}}e^{-i\langle\hbar^{-1/d}x_{m},p_{m}-p_{m-1}\rangle}\prod_{m=1}^{k_{2}}e^{i\langle\hbar^{-1/d}\tilde{x}_{m},q_{m}-q_{m-1}\rangle}f(\bm{p})g(\bm{q})\,d\bm{x}d\bm{\tilde{x}}d\bm{p}d\bm{q}.

We can write the phase functions just involving
\bm{x}
in the following way

\displaystyle\prod_{m=1}^{k_{1}}e^{-i\langle\hbar^{-1/d}x_{m},p_{m}-p_{m-1}\rangle}=\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}e^{-i\hbar^{-1/d}\langle x_{\sigma^{1}_{i}},p_{\sigma^{1}_{i}}\rangle}e^{i\hbar^{-1/d}\langle x_{\sigma^{1}_{i-1}+1},p_{\sigma^{1}_{i-1}}\rangle}\prod_{m=\sigma^{1}_{i-1}+1}^{\sigma^{1}_{i}-1}e^{-i\hbar^{-1/d}\langle x_{m}-x_{m+1},p_{m}\rangle},

where we have used the conventions that
\sigma^{1}_{0}=0
and
\sigma^{1}_{n+1}=k_{1}
. In the cases where
\sigma^{1}_{n}=k_{1}
the last factor in the product over
i
is identical 1. The phases functions involving
\bm{\tilde{x}}
can be written in an analogous way.
Next for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n+1\}
we do the following change of variables

\displaystyle x_{\sigma^{1}_{i}}\mapsto x_{\sigma^{1}_{i}}\quad\text{and}\quad x_{m}\mapsto x_{m}-x_{m+1}\quad\text{for all $m\in\{\sigma^{1}_{{i}-1}+1,\dots,{\sigma^{1}_{i}-1}\}$}

\displaystyle\tilde{x}_{\sigma^{2}_{i}}\mapsto\tilde{x}_{\sigma^{2}_{i}}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{x}_{m}\mapsto\tilde{x}_{m}-\tilde{x}_{m+1}\quad\text{for all $m\in\{\sigma^{2}_{{i}-1}+1,\dots,{\sigma^{2}_{i}-1}\}$}.

After this change of variables we evaluate all integrals in
\bm{\tilde{x}}
and all
\bm{x}
except
x_{\sigma_{i}^{1}}
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. This gives us the following expression

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{X},k_{1},k_{2})]=\frac{(\hbar\rho(2\pi)^{d})^{k_{1}+k_{2}-n}}{(2\pi)^{dn}}\int

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}e^{-i\langle x_{i},p_{\sigma^{1}_{i}}-p_{\sigma^{1}_{i-1}}-q_{\sigma^{2}_{\kappa(i)}}+q_{\sigma^{2}_{\kappa(i)-1}}\rangle}\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}\Big\{\prod_{m=\sigma^{1}_{i-1}+1}^{\sigma^{1}_{i}-1}\delta(p_{m}-p_{\sigma^{1}_{i-1}})

\displaystyle\times\prod_{m=\sigma^{2}_{i-1}+1}^{\sigma^{2}_{i}-1}\delta(q_{m}-q_{\sigma^{1}_{i-1}})\Big\}f(\bm{p})g(\bm{q})\,d\bm{x}d\bm{p}d\bm{q},

where we have done a relabelling and rescaling of the remaining
x
variables. To obtain the stated form in the Lemma we do the change of variables
x_{1}\mapsto x_{1}
and
x_{i}\mapsto x_{i}-x_{i-1}
for
i\in\{2,\dots,n\}
. This yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{X},k_{1},k_{2})]=\frac{(\hbar\rho(2\pi)^{d})^{k_{1}+k_{2}-n}}{(2\pi)^{dn}}\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}e^{i\langle x_{i},p_{\sigma^{1}_{i-1}}-q_{\sigma^{2}_{\kappa(i)-1}}-p_{\sigma^{1}_{n}}+q_{\sigma^{2}_{n}}-l^{\kappa}_{i}(\bm{q}_{\sigma^{2}})\rangle}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}\prod_{m=\sigma^{1}_{i-1}+1}^{\sigma^{1}_{i}-1}\delta(p_{m}-p_{\sigma^{1}_{i-1}})\prod_{m=\sigma^{2}_{i-1}+1}^{\sigma^{2}_{i}-1}\delta(q_{m}-q_{\sigma^{1}_{i-1}})f(\bm{p})g(\bm{q})\,d\bm{x}d\bm{p}d\bm{q},

where we have used the notation
l^{\kappa}_{i}(\bm{q}_{\sigma^{2}})=\sum_{j=i+1}^{n+1}(q_{\sigma^{2}_{\kappa(j)-1}}-q_{\sigma^{2}_{\kappa(j-1)}})
. To obtain the result we evaluate the remaining integrals in
x
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Before the next Lemma we will need the following notation. We set

\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{k}=\{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in\mathcal{X}^{k}:\text{$x_{i}\neq x_{j}$ for all $i\neq j$}\}.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
f
a functions such that
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{(k+1)d})
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\big\lVert\sum_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{k}}f(\bm{x},y)\big\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}_{y})}^{2}\Big]\leq 2\sum_{n=0}^{k}\binom{k}{n}n!\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{A}(k,n)}\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{(\bm{x},\bm{\tilde{x}})\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{2k}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\delta(x_{\sigma_{i}}-\tilde{x}_{\sigma_{i}})}{\rho}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\bm{x},y)\overline{f(\bm{\tilde{x}},y)}\,dy\Big].

Proof.

From writing out the
L^{2}
-norm we get that

\big\lVert\sum_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{k}}f(\bm{x},y)\big\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}_{y})}^{2}=\sum_{n=0}^{k}\sum_{\sigma^{1},\sigma^{2}\in\mathcal{A}(k,n)}\sum_{\kappa\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}\sum_{(\bm{x},\bm{\tilde{x}})\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{2k}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\delta(x_{\sigma_{i}}-\tilde{x}_{\sigma_{\kappa(i)}})}{\rho}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\bm{x},y)\overline{f(\bm{\tilde{x}},y)}\,dy,

(2.26)

where we are summing over all possible ways we can pair the two collision series. By linearity of the average we will consider a fixed
n
,
\sigma^{1}
,
\sigma^{2}
and
\kappa
. Moreover, we will denote by
\mathcal{X}_{\sigma^{i}}
the points of the point process who’s index is given by the values of
\sigma^{i}
. Independence of the points of the point process gives us that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{(\bm{x},\bm{\tilde{x}})\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{2k}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\delta(x_{\sigma_{i}^{1}}-\tilde{x}_{\sigma_{\kappa(i)}^{2}})}{\rho}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\bm{x},y)\overline{f(\bm{\tilde{x}},y)}\,dy\Big]

(2.27)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big|\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}^{\neq}_{k}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{\sigma^{1}}}[f(\bm{x},y)]\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}^{\neq}_{k}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{\sigma^{2}}}[\overline{f(\bm{\tilde{x}},y)}]\big|\,dy\Big],

where we have used that the left hand side is a positive number and moved the absolute value under the integrals to obtain the inequality. Using that for
a,b\geq 0
we have the inequality
ab\leq a^{2}+b^{2}
we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big|\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}^{\neq}_{k}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{\sigma^{1}}}[f(\bm{x},y)]\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}^{\neq}_{k}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{\sigma^{2}}}[\overline{f(\bm{\tilde{x}},y)}]\big|\,dy\Big]

(2.28)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}}\big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big|\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}^{\neq}_{k-n}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{\sigma^{1}}}\big[f(\bm{x},y)\big]\big|^{2}+\big|\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}^{\neq}_{k-n}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{\sigma^{2}}}\big[\overline{f(\bm{x},y)}\big]\big|^{2}\,dy\big].

Using independence of the points in the point process again we get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}}\big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big|\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}^{\neq}_{k-n}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{\sigma^{1}}}\big[f(\bm{x},y)\big]\big|^{2}+\big|\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}^{\neq}_{k-n}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{\sigma^{2}}}\big[\overline{f(\bm{x},y))}\big]\big|^{2}\,dy\big]

(2.29)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{2}\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{(\bm{x},\bm{\tilde{x}})\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{2k}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\delta(x_{\sigma_{i}^{j}}-\tilde{x}_{\sigma_{i}^{j}})}{\rho}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\bm{x},y)\overline{f(\bm{\tilde{x}},y)}\,dy\Big].

From combining (
2.27
), (
2.28
) and (
2.29
) we obtain the estimate

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{(\bm{x},\bm{\tilde{x}})\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{2k}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\delta(x_{\sigma_{i}^{1}}-\tilde{x}_{\sigma_{\kappa(i)}^{2}})}{\rho}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\bm{x},y)\overline{f(\bm{\tilde{x}},y)}\,dy\Big]

(2.30)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{2}\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{(\bm{x},\bm{\tilde{x}})\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{2k}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\delta(x_{\sigma_{i}^{j}}-\tilde{x}_{\sigma_{i}^{j}})}{\rho}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\bm{x},y)\overline{f(\bm{\tilde{x}},y)}\,dy\Big].

Using that the number of elements in
\mathcal{S}_{n}
is
n!
and the number of elements in
\mathcal{A}(k,n)
is
\binom{k}{n}
combined with (
2.26
) and (
2.30
) we obtain the stated estimate.
∎

3
Duhamel expansion

We start this section by introducing the heuristics of the Duhamel expansion and then introduce functions and operators that will appear naturally in our expansion and establish basic properties of these.
We will then perform the Duhamel expansion. The convergence of the expansion will rely on some technical estimates that will be proved in the following sections. The convergence of the expansion in the limit will be proven as a part of the proof for our main theorem. The difficulties in obtaining the convergence in the limit is that we need uniform bounds in
\hbar
or bounds that goes to zero as
\hbar
goes to zero. If we only needed the convergence for
\hbar
fixed, it would be “easier” to obtain the convergence but the error terms would divergence as
\hbar
goes to zero.

The expansions builds on the fundamental theorem of calculus which gives us the Duhamel formula

\displaystyle U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-t)U_{\hbar,0}(t)-I

\displaystyle=\frac{i\lambda}{\hbar}\int_{0}^{t}U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-t_{1})W_{\hbar}U_{\hbar,0}(-t_{1})\,d\bm{t}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{i\lambda}{\hbar}\int_{0}^{t}U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-t_{1})U_{\hbar,0}(-t_{1})W_{\hbar}^{t_{1}}\,d\bm{t}_{1},

where we have used the notation
W_{\hbar}^{t_{1}}=U_{\hbar,0}(t_{1})W_{\hbar}U_{\hbar,0}(-t_{1})
. We will obtain our expansion by iterating the above procedure. We separate terms into diagonal and off-diagonal forms, e.g.

W_{\hbar}^{t_{2}}W_{\hbar}^{t_{1}}=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}V_{\hbar,x}^{t_{2}}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}V_{\hbar,x}^{t_{1}}=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}V_{\hbar,x}^{t_{2}}V_{\hbar,x}^{t_{1}}+\sum_{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{2}}V_{\hbar,x_{2}}^{t_{2}}V_{\hbar,x_{1}}^{t_{1}}.

In each step of our iteration we will preform this type of sorting, which produces all possible “collision series” up to that point. This is simply a list over the potentials we encounter, where we allow for repetitions. When consecutive potentials are located at the same point we call it “internal scattering”. We will in our expansion consider these as one collision event and when the next potential is located at another point as the next collision with a number of internal scatterings. We will denote the number of internal scatterings at the
i
’th collision by
\alpha_{i}
. For us a recollision will be if a potential at some location appears again non consecutively.

When we do these expansions we will continuously keep track of the collision series. If certain collision terms appear we will stop the expansion. To be precise we will stop when the third recollision appears in a collision series. But if we just do this for the full operator
U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-t)
we will not be able to control the error terms. So we will combine this with a time division argument. That is we write the operator
U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-t)
as a product of the following form

U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-t)=\prod_{\tau=1}^{\tau_{0}}U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-\tfrac{t}{\tau_{0}}),

then we will do the expansion for each operator in the product. This will produce some main terms and some error terms. Heuristically we will get that

U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-\tfrac{t}{\tau_{0}})=\sum\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{R}_{1},

where
I
is the sum over our main term and
\mathcal{R}_{1}
is the reminder. The explicit expressions for these terms will be given below. For the “next”
U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-\tfrac{t}{\tau_{0}})
we only expand if we have the main terms to the right. So we obtain that

U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-\tfrac{2t}{\tau_{0}})=\sum\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{R}_{2}+U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-\tfrac{1}{\tau_{0}})\mathcal{R}_{1}.

Iterating this process we obtain an expression of the form

U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-t)=\sum\mathcal{I}+\sum_{\tau=1}^{\tau_{0}}U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-\tfrac{t(\tau_{0}-\tau}{\tau_{0}})\mathcal{R}_{\tau}.

The reminder/error terms
\mathcal{R}_{\tau}
will not only contain terms where we have observed a third recollision but also terms, where we have seen more than
k_{0}
different collision centers in the time interval
[0,\frac{t}{\tau_{0}}]
. We will later choose the numbers
k_{0}
and
\tau_{0}
to be
\hbar
depended such that we can control the reminder/error terms.

As stated above we need some notation to keep track of these collision series and expansions. We will in the following subsection define the functions and operators, which we will use to keep track of the expansions and prove some basic properties of these.

3.1
Definitions and properties of functions and operators for Duhamel expansion

We start by defining a function that will appear in the kernel for the operator associated to the internal scattering events.

Definition 3.1
.

For
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
s\in[0,\infty]
,
\gamma\geq 0
and
V\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we define

\displaystyle\Psi_{m}^{\gamma}(p_{m},p_{0},s;V)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{dm}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m-1}}\int\bm{1}_{[0,s]}\big(\bm{t}_{1,m-1}^{+}\big)\hat{V}(p_{m}-p_{m-1})\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}e^{it_{i}\frac{1}{2}(p_{i}^{2}-p_{0}^{2}+i\gamma)}\hat{V}(p_{i}-p_{i-1})\,d\bm{p}_{1,m-1}d\bm{t}.

The parameter
\gamma
should be viewed as a regularisation parameter, which we will need later. We will in the following also use the convention that
\Psi_{m}^{0}=\Psi_{m}
. In later estimates it will be convenient to write the function
\Psi_{m}^{\gamma}(p_{m},p_{0},s;V)
as

\displaystyle\Psi_{m}^{\gamma}(p_{m},p_{0},s;V)={}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m-1}}\int\bm{1}_{[0,s]}\big(\bm{t}_{1,m-1}^{+}\big)\hat{\mathcal{V}}_{m}(p_{m},p_{0},\bm{p}_{1,m-1})e^{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma\bm{t}_{1,m-1}^{+}}\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}e^{i\frac{1}{2}t_{i}(p_{i}^{2}-p_{0}^{2})}\,d\bm{p}_{1,m-1}d\bm{t},

(3.1)

where the functions
\hat{\mathcal{V}}_{m}(p_{m},p_{0},\bm{p}_{1,m-1})
is defined by

\hat{\mathcal{V}}_{m}(p_{m},p_{0},\bm{p}_{1,m-1})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{dm}}\begin{cases}\hat{V}(p_{m}-p_{0})&\text{if $m=1$,}\\
\hat{V}(p_{m}-p_{m-1})\hat{V}(p_{1}-p_{0})\prod_{i=2}^{m-1}\hat{V}(p_{i}-p_{i-1})&\text{if $m>1$.}\end{cases}

(3.2)

Lemma 3.2
.

For
V\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\hbar\in(0,\hbar_{0}]
,
\gamma\geq 0
and
0\leq s
, we have

\int|\Psi_{m}^{\gamma}(p_{m},p_{0},s;V)|\,dp_{0/m}\leq\frac{C_{d}^{m-1}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\lVert\hat{V}\rVert_{1,\infty,2d+2}^{m},

\sup_{p_{m},p_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\Psi_{m}^{\gamma}(p_{m},p_{0},s;V)|\leq\frac{C_{d}^{m-1}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\lVert\hat{V}\rVert_{1,\infty,2d+2}^{m},

where
\,dp_{0/m}
means either the integral with respect to
p_{0}
or with respect to
p_{m}
. The bound is uniform for
\hbar\in[0,\hbar_{0}]
and
\gamma\geq 0
.

Proof.

We will prove the bound for the case where we integrate with respect to
p_{m}
. The other case is proven analogously. We have by the definition of
\Psi_{m}^{\gamma}
(Definition
3.1
) that

\displaystyle\int|\Psi_{m}^{\gamma}(p_{m},p_{0},s;V)|\,dp_{m}\leq\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{dm}}\int\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m-1}}\Big|\int\prod_{i=1}^{m}\hat{V}(p_{i}-p_{i-1})\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}e^{it_{i}\frac{1}{2}p_{i}^{2}}\,d\bm{p}_{1,m-1}\Big|d\bm{t}dp_{m}.

We note here the integral over the
p
’s are of the same form as in Lemma
2.2
. Hence we get the estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\Psi_{m}^{\gamma}(p_{m},p_{0},s;V)|\,dp_{m}\leq\frac{(d-2)^{m-1}}{d^{m-1}(2\pi)^{d}}C_{d}^{m-1}\lVert\hat{V}\rVert_{1,\infty,2d+2}^{m}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m-1}}\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}\frac{1}{\max(1,|t_{i}|)^{\frac{d}{2}}}d\bm{t}_{m-1,1}.

(3.3)

Moreover we have that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m-1}}\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}\frac{1}{\max(1,|t_{i}|)^{\frac{d}{2}}}d\bm{t}_{m-1,1}=\left(\frac{d}{d-2}\right)^{m-1}.

(3.4)

By combining (
3.3
) and (
3.4
) we obtain the first estimate. The second estimate is proven analogously. This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

Recall that the
T
-operator
T^{\gamma}(E)
is defined as the
\gamma\to 0_{+}
limit of

T^{\gamma}(E)=\lambda V+\lambda^{2}V\frac{1}{E-(-\frac{1}{2}\Delta+\lambda V)+i\gamma}V.

From the resolvent formalism we obtain the formal series expansion given by

T^{\gamma}(E)=\lambda V\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\big[\lambda\frac{1}{E-(-\frac{1}{2}\Delta)+i\gamma}V\big]^{n}.

We are interested in the kernel of the above expression in momentum representation and we denote this kernel by
\hat{T}^{\gamma}(p,p_{0})
, where we have set
E=\frac{1}{2}p_{0}^{2}
. When we do the same for the kernels in the formal expansion we observe that the kernel of the
n
’th operator is given by

i^{n-1}\Psi_{n}^{\gamma}(p,p_{0},\infty;V).

From this we get the formal expression for the kernel of the operator in momentum representation is given by

\hat{T}^{\gamma}(p,p_{0})=-i\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(i\lambda)^{n}\Psi_{n}^{\gamma}(p,p_{0},\infty;V).

(3.5)

Lemma
3.2
give us, under the condition
\lambda C_{d}\lVert\hat{V}\rVert_{1,\infty,2d+2}<1
, that the series in (
3.5
) converges in the strong operator topology, uniformly for all
\gamma\geq 0
.

The next definition is of the operators which describes the internal collisions.

Definition 3.4
.

For
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
s_{1}\geq s_{2}\geq 0
,
\hbar>0
,
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
V\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we define
\Theta_{m}(s_{1},{s}_{2},z;V,\hbar)
as the operator

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\hbar}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m-1}}\bm{1}_{[0,\hbar^{-1}(s_{1}-s_{2})]}\big(\bm{t}_{1,m-1}^{+}\big)U_{\hbar,0}(-s_{2})V_{\hbar,z}\Big\{\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}U_{\hbar,0}(-t_{i})V_{\hbar,z}\Big\}U_{\hbar,0}(\bm{t}_{1,m-1}^{+}+s_{2})\,d\bm{t}_{1,m-1},

where
V_{\hbar,z}
is defined in (
1.2
).

Remark 3.5
.

Note that the kernel of
\Theta_{m}(s_{1},{s}_{2},z;V,\hbar)
in momentum representation is given by

\displaystyle(p_{m},p_{0})\mapsto{}\frac{1}{\hbar}e^{-i\hbar^{-1/d}\langle z,p_{m}-p_{0}\rangle}e^{is_{2}\hbar^{-1}\frac{1}{2}(p_{m}^{2}-p_{0}^{2})}\Psi_{m}(p_{m},p_{0},\hbar^{-1}(s_{1}-s_{2});V),

(3.6)

where the functions
\Psi_{m}
is defined in Definition
3.1
. Using this observation, Lemma
3.2
and the Schur test we obtain that

\lVert\Theta_{m}(s_{1},{s}_{2},z;V,\hbar)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\hbar^{-1}C_{d}^{m-1}\sup_{|\beta|\leq 2d+2}\lVert\partial^{\beta}\hat{V}\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{m},

where
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}
, is the operator norm. We will in the following for
\gamma\geq 0
use the notation
\Theta_{m}^{\gamma}(s_{1},{s}_{2},z;V,\hbar)
for operators where the kernels have the same form as in (
3.6
) with
\Psi_{m}
replaced by
\Psi_{m}^{\gamma}
.

We will now start to define the operators associated to fully expanded and truncated terms. We start with terms without recollisions.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
H_{\hbar,\lambda}=-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\Delta+\lambda W_{\hbar}(x)
, where
W_{\hbar}
and
\lambda
satisfy Assumption (
1.16
),
\hbar\in(0,\hbar_{0}]
and let
U_{\hbar,\lambda}(t)=e^{-it\hbar^{-1}H_{\hbar,\lambda}}
.
For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\gamma\geq 0
and
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{k}
we define the operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{0,0}^{\gamma}(k,\bm{x},t;\hbar)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k}}(i\lambda)^{|\alpha|}\int_{[0,t]_{\leq}^{k}}\prod_{m=1}^{k}\Theta_{\alpha_{m}}^{\gamma}(s_{m-1},{s}_{m},x_{m};V,\hbar)\,d\bm{s}_{k,1}U_{\hbar,0}(-t).

We will use the convention
\mathcal{I}_{0,0}^{0}(k,\bm{x},t;\hbar)=\mathcal{I}_{0,0}(k,\bm{x},t;\hbar)
. For a number
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{k_{0}+1}
we define the operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{k_{0}}_{0,0}(\bm{x},t;\hbar)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k_{0}}\times\{1\}}(i\lambda)^{|\alpha|}\int_{0}^{t}U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-s_{k_{0}+1})U_{\hbar,0}(s_{k_{0}+1})\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{k_{0}}(\bm{x},s_{k_{0}+1},\alpha,t;\hbar)\,ds_{k_{0}+1},

where

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{k_{0}}_{0,0}(\bm{x},s_{k_{0}+1},\alpha,t;\hbar)=\int_{[0,t]_{\leq}^{k_{0}}}\bm{1}_{[s_{k_{0}+1},t]}(s_{k_{0}})\prod_{m=1}^{k_{0}+1}\Theta_{\alpha_{m}}(s_{m-1},{s}_{m},x_{m};V,\hbar)\,d\bm{s}_{k_{0},1}U_{\hbar,0}(-t).

Remark 3.7
.

Note that the operators just defined is indeed bounded operators. To see this use Remark
3.5
and Assumption (
1.16
) to obtain the the sum congresses absolutely in the space of bounded operators. The same will be true for the operators defined in Definition
3.12
and Definition
3.13
.

Before we define the operators associated fully expanded and truncated terms, where we have recollisions, we need some notation to keep track of in which position we have the recollision. We do this by defining the following classes of maps. Firstly the class describing one recollision.

Definition 3.8
.

For any
k\geq 3
in
\mathbb{N}
we let
\mathcal{Q}_{k,1,1}
be the set of maps
\iota:\{1,\dots,k\}\mapsto\{1,\dots,k-1\}
such that

\iota(m)=\begin{cases}m&\text{if $m<m_{2}$}\\
m_{1}&\text{if $m=m_{2}$}\\
m-1&\text{if $m>m_{2}$},\end{cases}

where
m_{1}\in\{1,\dots,k-2\}
and
m_{2}\in\{m_{1}+2,\dots,k\}
. Moreover, to each map
\iota
we associate
\iota^{*}
defined by

\iota^{*}(m)=\begin{cases}m&\text{if $m<m_{2}$}\\
m+1&\text{if $m\geq m_{2}$}.\end{cases}

For two recollisions we can either recollide two times at the same position or we can have two recollision with different potentials. We will distinguish these two scenarios by defining two diffrent classes of maps. The fist class is when we recollide twice at the same scatterer

Definition 3.9
.

For any
k\geq 5
in
\mathbb{N}
we let
\mathcal{Q}_{k,2,1}
be the set of maps
\iota:\{1,\dots,k\}\mapsto\{1,\dots,k-2\}
such that

\iota(m)=\begin{cases}m&\text{if $m<m_{2}$}\\
m_{1}&\text{if $m=m_{2}$}\\
m-1&\text{if $m_{2}<m<m_{3}$}\\
m_{1}&\text{if $m=m_{3}$}\\
m-2&\text{if $m>m_{3}$},\end{cases}

where
m_{1}\in\{1,\dots,k-4\}
,
m_{2}\in\{m_{1}+2,\dots,k-2\}
and
m_{3}\in\{m_{2}+2,\dots,k\}
. Moreover, to each map
\iota
we associate
\iota^{*}
defined by

\iota^{*}(m)=\begin{cases}m&\text{if $m<m_{2}$}\\
m+1&\text{if $m_{2}\leq m<m_{3}$}\\
m+2&\text{if $m\geq m_{3}$}.\end{cases}

Definition 3.10
.

For any
k\geq 4
in
\mathbb{N}
we let
\mathcal{Q}_{k,2,2}
be the set of maps
\iota:\{1,\dots,k\}\mapsto\{1,\dots,k-2\}
such that

\iota(m)=\begin{cases}m&\text{if $m<m_{1,2}$}\\
m_{1,1}&\text{if $m=m_{1,2}$}\\
m-1&\text{if $m_{1,2}<m<m_{2,2}$}\\
m_{2,1}&\text{if $m=m_{2,2}$}\\
m-2&\text{if $m>m_{2,2}$},\end{cases}

where
m_{1,1}\in\{1,\dots,k-2\}
,
m_{1,2}\in\{m_{1,1}+2,\dots,k-2\}
,
m_{2,1}\in\{1,\dots,k-2\}\setminus\{m_{1,1},m_{1,2}\}
and
m_{2,2}\in\{\min(m_{2,1}+2,m_{1,2}+1),\dots,k\}
. Moreover, to each map
\iota
we associate
\iota^{*}
defined by

\iota^{*}(m)=\begin{cases}m&\text{if $m<m_{1,2}$}\\
m+1&\text{if $m_{1,2}\leq m<m_{2,2}$}\\
m+2&\text{if $m\geq m_{2,2}$}.\end{cases}

Remark 3.11
.

In the following analysis we will need to understand how many maps are in
Q_{k,i,j}
for
(i,j)\in\{(0,0),(1,1),(2,1),(2,2)\}
and each
k
, where
Q_{k,0,0}=\{\mathrm{id}\}
. By counting the maps in each set we get that

\#Q_{k,i,j}=\begin{cases}1&\text{$(i,j)=(0,0)$ and $k\geq 1$}\\
\frac{(k-2)(k-1)}{2}&\text{$(i,j)=(1,1)$ and $k\geq 3$}\\
\frac{(k-4)(k-3)(k-2)}{6}&\text{$(i,j)=(2,1)$ and $k\geq 5$}\\
\frac{3k^{4}-30k^{2}+117k^{2}-210k+144}{24}&\text{$(i,j)=(2,2)$ and $k\geq 4$},\end{cases}

where
\#Q_{k,i,j}
is notation for the number of elements in the set
Q_{k,i,j}
.

For the operators associated with fully expanded and truncated terms with recollisions we have the following definition:

Definition 3.12
.

Let
H_{\hbar,\lambda}=-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\Delta+\lambda W_{\hbar}(x)
, where
W_{\hbar}
and
\lambda
satisfy Assumption (
1.16
),
\hbar\in(0,\hbar_{0}]
and let
U_{\hbar,\lambda}(t)=e^{-it\hbar^{-1}H_{\hbar,\lambda}}
. For
(i,j)\in\{(1,1),(2,1),(2,2)\}
,
k\geq k_{ij}
in
\mathbb{N}
,
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{k-i}
and
\iota\in\mathcal{Q}_{k,i,j}
we define the operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{i,j}(k,\bm{x},\iota,t;\hbar)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k}}(i\lambda)^{|\alpha|}\int_{[0,t]_{\leq}^{k}}\prod_{m=1}^{k}\Theta_{\alpha_{m}}(s_{m-1},{s}_{m},x_{\iota(m)};V,\hbar)\,d\bm{s}_{k,1}U_{\hbar,0}(-t),

where
k_{11}=3
,
k_{21}=5
and
k_{22}=4
. For a number
(i,j)\in\{(1,1),(2,1),(2,2)\}
,
k_{0}\geq 5
in
\mathbb{N}
,
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{k_{0}+1-i}
and
\iota\in\mathcal{Q}_{k_{0}+1,i,j}
we define the operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{i,j}^{k_{0}}(\bm{x},\iota,t;\hbar)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k_{0}}\times\{1\}}(i\lambda)^{|\alpha|}\int_{0}^{t}U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-s_{k_{0}})U_{\hbar,0}(s_{k_{0}})\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{i,j}^{k_{0}}(\bm{x},s_{k_{0}},\alpha,\iota,t;\hbar)\,ds_{k_{0}},

where

\displaystyle\ \tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{i,j}^{k_{0}}(\bm{x},s_{k_{0}},\alpha,\iota,t;\hbar)=\int_{[0,t]_{\leq}^{k_{0}}}\bm{1}_{[s_{k_{0}},t]}(s_{k_{0}-1})\prod_{m=1}^{k_{0}+1}\Theta_{\alpha_{m}}(s_{m-1},{s}_{m},x_{\iota(m)};V,\hbar)\,d\bm{s}_{k_{0}-1,1}U_{\hbar,0}(-t).

Next definition is of the truncated terms where we have encountered a third recollision.

Definition 3.13
.

Let
H_{\hbar,\lambda}=-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\Delta+\lambda W_{\hbar}(x)
, where
W_{\hbar}
and
\lambda
satisfy Assumption (
1.16
),
\hbar\in(0,\hbar_{0}]
and let
U_{\hbar,\lambda}(t)=e^{-it\hbar^{-1}H_{\hbar,\lambda}}
. For
j\in\{1,2\}
, any
k,k_{1}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{k_{1}+k-2}
and
\iota\in\mathcal{Q}_{k_{1}+k,2,j}
, where we assume
k_{1}+k\geq 6-j
we define the operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2,j}^{\mathrm{rec}}(k,k_{1},\bm{x},t;\hbar)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\hbar^{-1}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k}}(i\lambda)^{|\alpha|+1}\int_{0}^{t}U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-s_{k+1})U_{\hbar,0}(s_{k+1})\sum_{l=1,\iota(l)\neq k_{1}+k}^{k_{1}+k-2}\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{2,j}^{\mathrm{rec}}(s_{k+1},k,\bm{x},\alpha,l,t,\iota;\hbar)\,ds_{k+1},

where

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{2,j}^{\mathrm{rec}}(s_{k+1},k,\bm{x},\alpha,l,t,\iota;\hbar)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\int_{[0,t]_{\leq}^{k}}\bm{1}_{[s_{k+1},t]}(s_{k})V^{s_{k+1}}_{\hbar,x_{l}}\prod_{m=1}^{k}\Theta_{\alpha_{m}}(s_{m-1},{s}_{m},x_{\iota(m+k_{1})};V,\hbar)\,d\bm{s}_{k,1}U_{\hbar,0}(-t).

As described above we will not only preform one Duhamel expansion but do a time division and for each part we will preform the duhamel expansion. These last two definitions will define the different error operators that will appear when combining our stopping rules and the time division.

Definition 3.14
.

Assume we are in the same setting as Definition
3.6
and Definition
3.12
. For numbers
\tau_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
,
k_{0}\geq 5
and
(i,j)\in\{(0,0),(1,1),(2,1),(2,2)\}
we define the operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{1,i,j}^{k_{0}}(\hbar)=\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{Q}_{k_{0}+1,i,j}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{1}\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{k_{0}+1-i}}\mathcal{E}_{i,j}^{k_{0}}(\bm{x}_{1},\iota,\tfrac{t}{\tau_{0}};\hbar).

For
\tau\in\{2,\dots,\tau_{0}\}
we define the operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\tau,i,j}^{k_{0}}(N;\hbar)=\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{Q}_{k_{0}+1,i,j}}\sum_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{k_{0}+1-i}}\mathcal{E}_{i,j}^{k_{0}}(\bm{x},\iota,\tfrac{t}{\tau_{0}};\hbar)U_{\hbar,0}(-\tfrac{\tau-1}{\tau_{0}}t;\hbar)

\displaystyle+\sum_{k_{2}=1}^{k_{0}}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{Q}_{k_{0}+k_{2}+1,i,j}}\sum_{(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{k_{0}+k_{2}+1-i}}\mathcal{E}_{i,j}^{k_{0}}(\bm{x}_{1},\iota,\tfrac{t}{\tau_{0}};\hbar)\mathcal{I}_{i,j}(k_{2},\bm{x}_{2},\iota,\tfrac{\tau-1}{\tau_{0}}t;\hbar)

\displaystyle+\sum_{k_{2}=1}^{k_{0}}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{Q}_{k_{0}+k_{2}+1,i,j}}\sum_{(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{k_{0}+k_{2}+1-i}}\mathcal{E}_{i,j}^{k_{0}+1}((x_{2,k_{2}},\bm{x}_{1}),\iota,\tfrac{t}{\tau_{0}};\hbar)\mathcal{I}_{i,j}(k_{2},\bm{x}_{2},\iota,\tfrac{\tau-1}{\tau_{0}}t;\hbar)

\displaystyle+\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{k_{0}}\sum_{k_{2}=k_{0}-k_{1}+1}^{k_{0}}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{Q}_{k_{1}+k_{2},i,j}}\sum_{(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{k_{1}+k_{2}-i}}\mathcal{I}_{i,j}(k_{1},\bm{x}_{1},\iota,\tfrac{t}{\tau_{0}};\hbar)\mathcal{I}_{i,j}(k_{2},\bm{x}_{2},\iota,\tfrac{\tau-1}{\tau_{0}}t;\hbar)

\displaystyle+\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{k_{0}}\sum_{k_{2}=k_{0}-k_{1}+1}^{k_{0}}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{Q}_{k_{1}+k_{2},i,j}}\sum_{(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{k_{1}+k_{2}-i}}\mathcal{I}_{i,j}(k_{1}+1,(x_{2,k_{2}},\bm{x}_{1}),\iota,\tfrac{t}{\tau_{0}};\hbar)\mathcal{I}_{i,j}(k_{2},\bm{x}_{2},\iota,\tfrac{\tau-1}{\tau_{0}}t;\hbar),

where we use the convention that
\mathcal{Q}_{n,0,0}=\{\mathrm{id}\}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Definition 3.15
.

Assume we are in the same setting as Definition
3.13
. For numbers
\tau_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and
k_{0}\geq 5
in
\mathbb{N}
we define the operator

\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\mathrm{rec}}(k_{0};\hbar)=\sum_{j=1}^{2}\sum_{k_{1}=6-j}^{k_{0}}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{Q}_{k_{1}},2,j}\sum_{\bm{x}_{1}\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{k_{1}-2}}\mathcal{E}_{2,j}^{\mathrm{rec}}(k_{1},0,\bm{x}_{1},\iota,\tfrac{t}{\tau_{0}};\hbar).

For
\tau\in\{2,\dots,\tau_{0}\}
we define the operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\tau}^{\mathrm{rec}}(k_{0};\hbar)={}

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{2}\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{k_{0}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{0}}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{Q}_{k_{1}+k_{2},2,j}}\bm{1}_{\{k_{1}+k_{2}\geq 6-j\}}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}}\mathcal{E}_{2,j}^{\mathrm{rec}}(k_{1},k_{2},\bm{x}_{1},\iota,\tfrac{t}{\tau_{0}};\hbar)\mathcal{I}_{2,j}^{\mathrm{rec}}(k_{2},\bm{x}_{2},\iota,\tfrac{\tau-1}{\tau_{0}}t;\hbar)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{2}\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{k_{0}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{0}}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{Q}_{k_{1}+k_{2},2,j}}\bm{1}_{\{k_{1}+k_{2}\geq 6-j\}}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}}\mathcal{E}_{2,j}^{\mathrm{rec}}(k_{1}+1,k_{2},(x_{2,k_{2}},\bm{x}_{1}),\iota,\tfrac{t}{\tau_{0}};\hbar)\mathcal{I}_{2,j}^{\mathrm{rec}}(k_{2},\bm{x}_{2},\iota,\tfrac{\tau-1}{\tau_{0}}t;\hbar).

3.2
The Duhamel expansion

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
H_{\hbar,\lambda}=-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\Delta+\lambda W_{\hbar}(x)
, where
\hbar\in(0,\hbar_{0}]
,
W_{\hbar}
and
\lambda
satisfy Assumption (
1.16
) and let
U_{\hbar,\lambda}(t)=e^{-it\hbar^{-1}H_{\hbar,\lambda}}
. Then for any
\tau_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
we have that

U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-t)=U_{\hbar,0}(-t)+\sum_{i=0}^{2}\sum_{j=\min(1,i)}^{i}\sum_{k=k_{ij}}^{k_{0}}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{Q}_{k,i,j}}\sum_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{k-i}}\mathcal{I}_{i,j}(k,\bm{x},t;\hbar)+\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},k_{0}:\hbar),

where
k_{00}=1
,
k_{11}=3
,
k_{21}=5
,
k_{22}=4
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},k_{0}:\hbar)=\sum_{\tau=1}^{\tau_{0}}U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-\tfrac{\tau_{0}-\tau}{\tau_{0}}t)\big[\mathcal{R}_{\tau}^{\mathrm{rec}}(k_{0};\hbar)+\sum_{i=0}^{2}\sum_{j=\min(1,i)}^{i}\mathcal{R}_{\tau,i,j}^{k_{0}}(N;\hbar)\big].

The operators
\mathcal{I}_{i,j}(k,\bm{x},\alpha,t;\hbar)
are defined in Definition
3.6
and
3.12
. The remainder operators are defined in Definition
3.14
and
3.15
respectively.

The proof of this Lemma is essentially a carefully done Duhamel expansion, where we at all time keep track of the different collision series. However we do combine this with a time division argument. This combination is the driving force between the slightly more complicated error terms appearing.

Proof.

From the self adjointness of
H_{\hbar,\lambda}=-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\Delta+\lambda W_{\hbar}(x)
we have that
U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-t)
is a semigroup parametrised by
t
. Hence we have the following decomposition

U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-t)=\prod_{\tau=1}^{\tau_{0}}U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-\tfrac{t}{\tau_{0}})=U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-\tfrac{t(\tau_{0}-1)}{\tau_{0}})U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-\tfrac{t}{\tau_{0}}).

(3.7)

In the following we let
t_{0}=\frac{t}{\tau_{0}}
. From preforming a carefull Duhamel expansion as described in the start of this section we obtain the formal exspansion

\displaystyle U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-t_{0})=U_{\hbar,0}(-t_{0})+\sum_{i=0}^{2}\sum_{j=\min(1,i)}^{i}\sum_{k=k_{ij}}^{k_{0}}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{Q}_{k,i,j}}\sum_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{k-i}}\mathcal{T}(\iota,k,\bm{x},t_{0};\hbar)+\mathcal{D}_{1}^{1}(t_{0})+\mathcal{D}_{2}^{1}(t_{0}),

(3.8)

where the different terms
\mathcal{T}(\iota,k,\bm{x},t_{0};\hbar)
,
\mathcal{D}_{1}^{1}(t_{0})
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}^{1}(t_{0})
are given by

\mathcal{T}(\iota,k,\bm{x},t_{0};\hbar)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k}}\left(\frac{i\lambda}{\hbar}\right)^{|\alpha|}\int_{[0,t_{0}]_{\leq}^{|\alpha|}}\prod_{m=1}^{k}\prod_{i=\beta_{m-1}+1}^{\beta_{m}}V^{t_{i}}_{\hbar,x_{\iota(m)}}\,d\bm{t}_{|\alpha|,1}U_{\hbar,0}(-t_{0}).

(3.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{1}^{1}(t_{0})={}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{2}\sum_{j=\min(1,i)}^{i}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{Q}_{k_{0}+1,i,j}}\sum_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{k_{0}-i+1}}

(3.10)

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k_{0}}\times\{1\}}\left(\frac{i\lambda}{\hbar}\right)^{|\alpha|}\int_{[0,t_{0}]_{\leq}^{|\alpha|}}U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-t_{|\alpha|})U_{\hbar,0}(t_{|\alpha|})\prod_{m=1}^{k_{0}}\prod_{i=\beta_{m-1}+1}^{\beta_{m}}V^{t_{i}}_{\hbar,x_{\iota(m)}}\,d\bm{t}_{|\alpha|,1}U_{\hbar,0}(-t_{0}).

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{2}^{1}(t_{0})={}

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{2}\sum_{k=6-j}^{k_{0}}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{Q}_{k,2,j}}\sum_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{k-2}}\sum_{i=1,i\neq\iota(k)}^{k-2}

(3.11)

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k}}\left(\frac{i\lambda}{\hbar}\right)^{|\alpha|+1}\int_{[0,t_{0}]_{\leq}^{|\alpha|}}U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-t_{|\alpha|})U_{\hbar,0}(t_{|\alpha|})V^{t_{|\alpha|}}_{\hbar,x_{i}}\prod_{m=1}^{k}\prod_{i=\beta_{m-1}+1}^{\beta_{m}}V^{t_{i}}_{\hbar,x_{\iota(m)}}\,d\bm{t}_{|\alpha|,1}U_{\hbar,0}(-t_{0}).

Where we in the expression for the terms
\mathcal{T}(\iota,k,\bm{x},t_{0};\hbar)
,
\mathcal{D}_{1}^{1}(t_{0})
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}^{1}(t_{0})
have used the notation introduced in this section,
k
is the number of different scatterers we see, the entries of
\alpha
record the number of internal collisions for each scatterer, and finally the sum over
\bm{x}
is the sum over all possible configurations with different potentials. To compress the above expression we have introduced the notation
\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k+1}
to be given by
\beta_{0}=0
and
\beta_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{i}\alpha_{i}
.

It is here where we should have introduced an additional error that truncated the sum over
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
. We have chosen not to do this to simplify notation and presentation. Doing this more carefull expansion do not give rise to any technical difficulties.

For the two terms
\mathcal{D}_{1}^{1}(t_{0})
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}^{1}(t_{0})
we make the following change of variables
t_{\beta_{m}}\mapsto s_{m,\beta_{m}}
and
t_{i}-t_{i+1}\mapsto s_{m,i}
for
i\in\{\beta_{m-1}+1,\dots,\beta_{m}-1\}

m\in\{1,\dots,k\}
. With this change of variables we have for the term
\mathcal{D}_{1}^{1}(t_{0})

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{1}^{1}(t_{0})={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k_{0}}\times\{1\}}\left(\frac{i\lambda}{\hbar}\right)^{|\alpha|}\int_{[0,t_{0}]^{|\alpha|}}\bm{1}_{[0,t]^{k}_{\leq}}(s_{k,\beta_{k}},\dots,s_{1,\beta_{1}})U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-s_{k,\beta_{k}})U_{\hbar,0}(s_{k,\beta_{k}})

(3.12)

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle\times\Big[\prod_{m=1}^{k}\bm{1}_{[0,s_{m-1,\beta_{m-1}}-s_{m,\beta_{m}}]}\big(\sum_{i=\beta_{m-1}+1}^{\beta_{m}-1}s_{m,i}\big)\Big\{\prod_{i=\beta_{m-1}+1}^{\beta_{m}}U_{\hbar,0}(-s_{m,\beta_{i}}))V_{\hbar,x_{m}}\Big\}

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle\times\Big\{\prod_{i=\beta_{m-1}+1}^{\beta_{m}}U_{\hbar,0}(s_{m,i}))\Big\}\Big]\,\prod_{m=1}^{k}d\bm{s}_{m,(\beta_{m-1}+1,\beta_{m})}U_{\hbar,0}(-t_{0})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{k_{0}}_{0,0}(\bm{x},t_{0};\hbar),

where we have used Definition
3.4
and Definition
3.6
to recognise different terms. For
\mathcal{D}_{2}^{1}(t_{0})
we have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{2}^{1}(t_{0})={}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1,i\neq\iota(k)}^{k-2}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{k}}\left(\frac{i\lambda}{\hbar}\right)^{|\alpha|+1}\int_{[0,t_{0}]^{|\alpha|}}\bm{1}_{[0,t]^{k}_{\leq}}(s_{k,\beta_{k}},\dots,s_{1,\beta_{1}})U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-s_{k,\beta_{k}})U_{\hbar,0}(s_{k,\beta_{k}})V^{t_{|\alpha|}}_{\hbar,x_{i}}

(3.13)

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle\times\Big[\prod_{m=1}^{k}\bm{1}_{[0,s_{m-1,\beta_{m-1}}-s_{m,\beta_{m}}]}\big(\sum_{i=\beta_{m-1}+1}^{\beta_{m}-1}s_{m,i}\big)\Big\{\prod_{i=\beta_{m-1}+1}^{\beta_{m}}U_{\hbar,0}(-s_{m,\beta_{i}}))V_{\hbar,x_{m}}\Big\}

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle\times\Big\{\prod_{i=\beta_{m-1}+1}^{\beta_{m}}U_{\hbar,0}(s_{m,i}))\Big\}\Big]\,\prod_{m=1}^{k}d\bm{s}_{m,(\beta_{m-1}+1,\beta_{m})}U_{\hbar,0}(-t_{0})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2,j}^{\mathrm{rec}}(k,0,\bm{x},t_{0};\hbar),

where we have used Definition
3.4
and Definition
3.13
to recognise different terms. Combining (
3.7
)–(
3.13
) we obtain that

\displaystyle U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-t)={}

\displaystyle U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-\tfrac{t(\tau_{0}-1)}{\tau_{0}})\Big[U_{\hbar,0}(-t_{0})+\sum_{i=0}^{2}\sum_{j=\min(1,i)}^{i}\sum_{k=k_{ij}}^{k_{0}}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{Q}_{k,i,j}}\sum_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}_{\neq}^{k-i}}\mathcal{T}(\iota,k,\bm{x},t_{0};\hbar)\Big]

\displaystyle+U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-\tfrac{t(\tau_{0}-1)}{\tau_{0}})\big[\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\mathrm{rec}}(k_{0};\hbar)+\sum_{i=0}^{2}\sum_{j=\min(1,i)}^{i}\mathcal{R}_{1,i,j}^{k_{0}}(N;\hbar)\big],

where we have used Definition
3.14
and Definition
3.15
to recognise terms.
The next step is to iterate the argument we have just done. Firstly we write
U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-\tfrac{t(\tau_{0}-1)}{\tau_{0}})
as
U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-\tfrac{t(\tau_{0}-2)}{\tau_{0}})U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-\tfrac{t}{\tau_{0}})
and then we do the same Duhamel expansion of
U_{\hbar,\lambda}(-\tfrac{t}{\tau_{0}})
as we have already done. However this time we also need to keep the collision series to the right in mind. Especially we have to be carefully if the point we ended the first expansion with is the one we start with again. Making this Duhamel expansion we obtain that
